# A spill slot past the scaled imm12

## Documentation

AArch64's `LDR`/`STR` unsigned-offset form does not carry a byte displacement. It carries a
**12-bit count of ACCESS UNITS**, so a 64-bit spill slot reaches `4095 * 8 = 32760` bytes off
the frame pointer and not one byte further. `arm64SlotDisplacement` is `16 + slot * 8`, so
**slot 4094 is the first one with no form** — `16 + 4094 * 8 = 32768`, whose scaled count is
`4096`, one past the field.

x64 has no such ceiling in any reachable range: `[rsp + disp32]` takes a full 32-bit signed
byte displacement directly, so `slotDisplacement` is a plain `Imm32` and the only limit it
acknowledges is `i32.max`. wasm addresses locals by index and has no frame at all. **The
ceiling is one target's, and it is the emitter that has to know it.**

⚠ **The cure the sibling ceilings took is not available here.** `wide-field-offsets.md`'s
struct-field displacement is a compile-time constant at instruction selection, so the ISel
folds it into the scratch base itself (`StdToArm64Conversion.addressFor`). A spill SLOT
number does not exist at ISel — it is minted by the splitter, inside register allocation,
which runs after. There is nothing for an ISel-tier fold to fold.

⚠ **And the scratch register cannot be x16.** The ISel mints `x16` producer/consumer pairs
that later passes demonstrably splice ops between; a spill or a reload is exactly such a
spliced op, so an encoder-tier `x16` materialisation for spill addressing would clobber a
pending fold. `x17` — the other AAPCS64 IP scratch, outside the allocatable pool, and used
today only inside a single terminator (`arm64JumpTable`, which declares both) — is the one
register no post-ISel splice can be sitting inside.

⚠ **AND THE SPILL SLOT IS NOT THE ONLY FRAME REFERENCE THAT OUTRUNS THE FIELD.** An INCOMING
STACK ARGUMENT is read at `[x29 + frameSize - outgoingArgSize + slot*8]`, a displacement the
whole frame contributes to — so it passes the ceiling *sooner* than any spill slot does, and it
is `Arm64PrologueEpilogue` rather than the encoder that mints it (only that pass knows the frame
size). It folds the same way, into the same `x17`, through the same split.

## Tests

<!-- test: spill-slot-past-the-scaled-imm12 -->
`sink(3)` is `4`, so `v0`..`v4110` are `5`..`4115` and their sum is
`8468660`. Nothing about the arithmetic is the point: the point is
**4111 values live at one program point**, which forces the splitter to spill
4094 distinct values, whose highest slot is **4094** — the first with no
scaled-imm12 form. It is the smallest program that reaches it: 4109 values stop
one slot short and compile clean on every target.

```maxon
typealias Integer = int(i64.min to i64.max)

function sink(x Integer) returns Integer
	return x + 1
end 'sink'

	let n = sink(3)
```

<!-- test: wide-incoming-argument-past-the-scaled-imm12 -->
`wide`'s ten arguments sum to `55`, so `v0`..`v4110` are `56`..`4166` and their
sum is `8678321`. The arithmetic is again not the point: the point is that
**`a8` and `a9` travel on the STACK** (AAPCS64 passes the first eight of a file in
registers), so `wide` reads them at `[x29 + frameSize - outgoingArgSize + slot*8]`
— and its 4111 live values put `frameSize` past the scaled field, so that
displacement has no direct form either. It is a SECOND door on the same ceiling,
in a second pass, and the first case cannot reach it: a function with eight
arguments or fewer reads none of them from the caller's frame.

```maxon
typealias Integer = int(i64.min to i64.max)

function wide(a0 Integer, a1 Integer, a2 Integer, a3 Integer, a4 Integer, a5 Integer, a6 Integer, a7 Integer, a8 Integer, a9 Integer) returns Integer
	let n = a0 + a1 + a2 + a3 + a4 + a5 + a6 + a7 + a8 + a9
	let v0 = n + 1
	let v1 = n + 2
	let v2 = n + 3
	let v3 = n + 4
	let v4 = n + 5
	let v5 = n + 6
	let v6 = n + 7
	let v7 = n + 8
	let v8 = n + 9
	let v9 = n + 10
	let v10 = n + 11
	let v11 = n + 12
	let v12 = n + 13
	let v13 = n + 14
	let v14 = n + 15
	let v15 = n + 16
	let v16 = n + 17
	let v17 = n + 18
	let v18 = n + 19
	let v19 = n + 20
	let v20 = n + 21
	let v21 = n + 22
	let v22 = n + 23
	let v23 = n + 24
	let v24 = n + 25
	let v25 = n + 26
	let v26 = n + 27
```
